========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the rat itself, I think. I mean, I know people talk about germs and diseases and all that, and sure, that doesn’t sound good, but that’s not really the main thing in my mind when it happens. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.

At work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It’s not just at work, even though work is where it causes the biggest problems. In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.

Like if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.

And if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.

It’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.

Mice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.

So maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.

In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.\n\nMice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.

Like if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.

So I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.

It’s gotten so bad that I’m avoiding certain areas or I just can’t make myself go in, and that’s a real problem because cleaning is my job.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the rats themselves that you're afraid of, or is it more about the potential diseases or harm they might bring?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself, I think. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.\n\nAt work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there areas in your personal life, outside of work, that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s gotten kind of out of hand with rats. Just the thought that I might see one, like in a trash room or an old part of a building, is enough to make me not able to do parts of my cleaning job. And I know, I do know, rationally, that it’s usually just an old building or a messy area and it’s probably not actually dangerous, but it doesn’t feel that way to me in the moment. It’s gotten so bad that I’m avoiding certain areas or I just can’t make myself go in, and that’s a real problem because cleaning is my job.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the rats themselves that you're afraid of, or is it more about the potential diseases or harm they might bring?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself, I think. I mean, I know people talk about germs and diseases and all that, and sure, that doesn’t sound good, but that’s not really the main thing in my mind when it happens. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.\n\nAt work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there areas in your personal life, outside of work, that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just at work, even though work is where it causes the biggest problems. In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.\n\nMice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by seeing a picture or a toy representation of a rat, or is it specifically the thought of a live rat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.\n\nLike if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.\n\nAnd I know, logically, a lot of the time it’s probably just an old building being an old building, or a trash area that has smells and noises and things like that. I clean for work, so I’m around those kinds of places, and I tell myself, “This doesn’t mean there’s a rat here.” But the thought of seeing one has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.

It’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.

So with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.

It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.

My supervisor has started noticing. It’s not like they’ve said outright, “You’re in trouble,” but I can tell they’re questioning whether they can count on me for regular routes. And that scares me because I need my job. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work. I’ve always tried to be dependable.

It’s also upsetting because there are better shifts that come up, and I’ve thought about trying for a lead position someday, but now I worry this is going to cost me that. Like, if they think I can’t handle certain buildings, why would they give me more responsibility? So even if it starts as “just” being afraid of rats, it ends up following me home because I’m thinking about whether I messed up at work, whether my supervisor is annoyed, whether I’m ruining chances I worked hard for.

With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by seeing a picture or a toy representation of a rat, or is it specifically the thought of a live rat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.\n\nLike if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.\n\nAnd I know, logically, a lot of the time it’s probably just an old building being an old building, or a trash area that has smells and noises and things like that. I clean for work, so I’m around those kinds of places, and I tell myself, “This doesn’t mean there’s a rat here.” But the thought of seeing one has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your fear is specifically of rats, but does the fear extend to other similar rodents like squirrels or gerbils?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.\n\nIt’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.\n\nSo with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.\n\nMy supervisor has started noticing. It’s not like they’ve said outright, “You’re in trouble,” but I can tell they’re questioning whether they can count on me for regular routes. And that scares me because I need my job. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work. I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nIt’s also upsetting because there are better shifts that come up, and I’ve thought about trying for a lead position someday, but now I worry this is going to cost me that. Like, if they think I can’t handle certain buildings, why would they give me more responsibility? So even if it starts as “just” being afraid of rats, it ends up following me home because I’m thinking about whether I messed up at work, whether my supervisor is annoyed, whether I’m ruining chances I worked hard for.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I don’t even need to see or hear a rat for it to start. If I’m in an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere that just looks like the kind of place rats could be, my mind goes right to, “What if one runs out?”

And I do know, at least in my head, that an old building or a trash room isn’t automatically dangerous. I can tell myself, “This is just part of the job, it’s probably nothing.” But the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning work anymore. I’ll hesitate at a doorway, or avoid moving things, or feel like I just can’t go into a certain area because I’m imagining one being there.

It’s frustrating because I know I’m reacting to the possibility more than anything actually happening, but it still feels like I can’t make myself do it.
=====================================

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s gotten kind of out of hand with rats. Just the thought that I might see one, like in a trash room or an old part of a building, is enough to make me not able to do parts of my cleaning job. And I know, I do know, rationally, that it’s usually just an old building or a messy area and it’s probably not actually dangerous, but it doesn’t feel that way to me in the moment. It’s gotten so bad that I’m avoiding certain areas or I just can’t make myself go in, and that’s a real problem because cleaning is my job.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the rats themselves that you're afraid of, or is it more about the potential diseases or harm they might bring?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself, I think. I mean, I know people talk about germs and diseases and all that, and sure, that doesn’t sound good, but that’s not really the main thing in my mind when it happens. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.\n\nAt work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there areas in your personal life, outside of work, that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just at work, even though work is where it causes the biggest problems. In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.\n\nMice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by seeing a picture or a toy representation of a rat, or is it specifically the thought of a live rat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.\n\nLike if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.\n\nAnd I know, logically, a lot of the time it’s probably just an old building being an old building, or a trash area that has smells and noises and things like that. I clean for work, so I’m around those kinds of places, and I tell myself, “This doesn’t mean there’s a rat here.” But the thought of seeing one has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your fear is specifically of rats, but does the fear extend to other similar rodents like squirrels or gerbils?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.\n\nIt’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.\n\nSo with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.\n\nMy supervisor has started noticing. It’s not like they’ve said outright, “You’re in trouble,” but I can tell they’re questioning whether they can count on me for regular routes. And that scares me because I need my job. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work. I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nIt’s also upsetting because there are better shifts that come up, and I’ve thought about trying for a lead position someday, but now I worry this is going to cost me that. Like, if they think I can’t handle certain buildings, why would they give me more responsibility? So even if it starts as “just” being afraid of rats, it ends up following me home because I’m thinking about whether I messed up at work, whether my supervisor is annoyed, whether I’m ruining chances I worked hard for.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, without actually seeing or hearing a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I’m here. I don’t even need to see or hear a rat for it to start. If I’m in an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere that just looks like the kind of place rats could be, my mind goes right to, “What if one runs out?”\n\nAnd I do know, at least in my head, that an old building or a trash room isn’t automatically dangerous. I can tell myself, “This is just part of the job, it’s probably nothing.” But the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning work anymore. I’ll hesitate at a doorway, or avoid moving things, or feel like I just can’t go into a certain area because I’m imagining one being there.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know I’m reacting to the possibility more than anything actually happening, but it still feels like I can’t make myself do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.\n\nMy supervisor has started noticing. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the rat itself — like the presence of it, or even the idea that I might see one. I don’t really sit there thinking, “Oh, it’s going to ruin my things,” or anything like that. It’s not that kind of fear.

I mean, if you asked me logically, I know an old building or a trash room doesn’t automatically mean there’s some serious danger. I can tell myself that. But when I’m standing there, about to go into a place like that for work, my mind just goes straight to, “What if there’s a rat? What if I see one?” And that thought alone can stop me. I’ll feel like I can’t do that part of the cleaning, even when nothing has actually happened.

So it’s not really about my belongings or even some specific thing I think the rat is going to do. It’s the thought of encountering one — seeing it, having it be there — that has gotten so bad it’s affecting my job.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… it’s gotten kind of out of hand with rats. Just the thought that I might see one, like in a trash room or an old part of a building, is enough to make me not able to do parts of my cleaning job. And I know, I do know, rationally, that it’s usually just an old building or a messy area and it’s probably not actually dangerous, but it doesn’t feel that way to me in the moment. It’s gotten so bad that I’m avoiding certain areas or I just can’t make myself go in, and that’s a real problem because cleaning is my job.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the rats themselves that you're afraid of, or is it more about the potential diseases or harm they might bring?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself, I think. I mean, I know people talk about germs and diseases and all that, and sure, that doesn’t sound good, but that’s not really the main thing in my mind when it happens. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.\n\nAt work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there areas in your personal life, outside of work, that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just at work, even though work is where it causes the biggest problems. In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.\n\nMice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by seeing a picture or a toy representation of a rat, or is it specifically the thought of a live rat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.\n\nLike if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.\n\nAnd I know, logically, a lot of the time it’s probably just an old building being an old building, or a trash area that has smells and noises and things like that. I clean for work, so I’m around those kinds of places, and I tell myself, “This doesn’t mean there’s a rat here.” But the thought of seeing one has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your fear is specifically of rats, but does the fear extend to other similar rodents like squirrels or gerbils?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.\n\nIt’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.\n\nSo with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.\n\nMy supervisor has started noticing. It’s not like they’ve said outright, “You’re in trouble,” but I can tell they’re questioning whether they can count on me for regular routes. And that scares me because I need my job. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work. I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nIt’s also upsetting because there are better shifts that come up, and I’ve thought about trying for a lead position someday, but now I worry this is going to cost me that. Like, if they think I can’t handle certain buildings, why would they give me more responsibility? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. So even if it starts as “just” being afraid of rats, it ends up following me home because I’m thinking about whether I messed up at work, whether my supervisor is annoyed, whether I’m ruining chances I worked hard for.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, without actually seeing or hearing a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll hesitate at a doorway, or avoid moving things, or feel like I just can’t go into a certain area because I’m imagining one being there.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I know I’m reacting to the possibility more than anything actually happening, but it still feels like I can’t make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats related to what they might do to you or your belongings, or is it solely the presence of the rat itself that scares you?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself — like the presence of it, or even the idea that I might see one. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly it’s been work relationships, if that counts. I haven’t really stopped seeing friends or anything like that because of rats, but with my supervisor and the people who have to assign the routes, it’s become a problem.

I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially in buildings where I’m worried there might be rats. And then that puts other people in a bad position, because someone still has to get the work done. I hate that. I don’t want coworkers thinking I’m just being difficult or trying to avoid the harder jobs.

My supervisor has started noticing, too. They haven’t fully confronted me, but I can tell they’re questioning whether I can be counted on for regular routes. That’s embarrassing, and it makes me anxious because I need this job. I’ve always tried to be dependable.

And I worry it’s going to affect my chances for better shifts, or any chance at a lead position later on. Like, if they think I can’t handle certain buildings, why would they trust me with more responsibility? So it’s not really social in the way of parties or family gatherings, but it has definitely affected relationships at work and how people may see me.

Just the thought that I might see one, like in a trash room or an old part of a building, is enough to make me not able to do parts of my cleaning job. And I know, I do know, rationally, that it’s usually just an old building or a messy area and it’s probably not actually dangerous, but it doesn’t feel that way to me in the moment. It’s gotten so bad that I’m avoiding certain areas or I just can’t make myself go in, and that’s a real problem because cleaning is my job.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the rats themselves that you're afraid of, or is it more about the potential diseases or harm they might bring?\n\nPatient: It’s more the rat itself, I think. I mean, I know people talk about germs and diseases and all that, and sure, that doesn’t sound good, but that’s not really the main thing in my mind when it happens. It’s the idea of seeing one — like even just thinking, “What if there’s a rat in that trash room?” — that’s what gets me stuck.\n\nAt work, if I’m supposed to clean somewhere that looks like the kind of place one might be, an old storage area or around garbage, I just… I can’t make myself go in sometimes. And I know that sounds extreme because, logically, I can tell myself it’s probably just an old building, or it’s just messy, and not actually dangerous right then. But my body doesn’t listen to that. The thought of seeing one is enough to make me avoid the whole area, and that’s the part that’s really messing with my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there areas in your personal life, outside of work, that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just at work, even though work is where it causes the biggest problems. In my regular life I sort of plan around it without even realizing I’m doing it sometimes.\n\nLike if I’m walking somewhere and I know one way goes past alley dumpsters, or behind restaurants, or those little trash areas behind buildings, I’ll take a different route even if it’s longer. If a building has a basement laundry room or a storage area that feels dark or closed-in, I really don’t want to go down there. I’ll avoid it if I can. Same with basements in general, or old storage rooms, places where there are boxes and clutter and you can’t see what’s behind things.\n\nAnd if I absolutely have to go into a place like that, I do things to make it feel less trapped. I keep the door open, I try not to go too far in, and I rush through whatever I’m doing. I’m not calm while I’m doing it, either — I’m tense the whole time, looking around, just wanting to get out as fast as possible. It’s embarrassing because I know other people just go in and do what they need to do, but for me it feels like I’m bracing myself the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats triggered by anything else besides seeing them or being in places where they might be?\n\nPatient: Yes. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. Like, I know mice are similar, and if one ran across the floor I’m sure I wouldn’t like it — I mean, most people wouldn’t, probably. But when I think about the thing that really freezes me up, it’s rats.\n\nMice feel… I don’t know, smaller, I guess, and I don’t have the same immediate picture in my head with them. With rats it’s like the word itself already makes me uncomfortable. If someone says there might be rats somewhere, that’s enough to make me not want to go near it. With mice, I can’t say I seek them out or anything, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding places just because I thought there might be a mouse there in the same way.\n\nSo maybe there’s some overlap, but no, not really similar — not as strong, anyway. Rats are the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by seeing a picture or a toy representation of a rat, or is it specifically the thought of a live rat?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the idea that it could be a real, live rat, if that makes sense. Like a picture or a toy might make me uncomfortable, especially if it looks realistic, but what really sets me off is when my brain thinks there might actually be one there.\n\nLike if I hear scratching near a wall, or I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I don’t clearly know what it is, my whole body reacts right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I get this feeling like I have to get away from that spot immediately, before I can even think straight or check what it was. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. Just being in the kind of place where I think rats could be — like an old building, or a trash room, or somewhere with garbage around — my mind goes straight there.\n\nAnd I know, logically, a lot of the time it’s probably just an old building being an old building, or a trash area that has smells and noises and things like that. I clean for work, so I’m around those kinds of places, and I tell myself, “This doesn’t mean there’s a rat here.” But the thought of seeing one has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your fear is specifically of rats, but does the fear extend to other similar rodents like squirrels or gerbils?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.\n\nIt’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.\n\nSo with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know? I’ve had to refuse certain cleaning assignments, or ask if I can do them later, especially if it’s in a building where I’m already worried there might be rats. And sometimes “later” turns into me still not being able to make myself do it, which is awful to admit.\n\nMy supervisor has started noticing. It’s not like they’ve said outright, “You’re in trouble,” but I can tell they’re questioning whether they can count on me for regular routes. And that scares me because I need my job. I don’t want to be seen as unreliable or difficult, because I’m not trying to get out of work. I’ve always tried to be dependable.\n\nIt’s also upsetting because there are better shifts that come up, and I’ve thought about trying for a lead position someday, but now I worry this is going to cost me that. It’s the thought of encountering one — seeing it, having it be there — that has gotten so bad it’s affecting my job.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social situations or relationships that you've avoided or struggled with because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s been work relationships, if that counts. It doesn’t even have to be an actual rat right in front of me. Sometimes it’s a sound, like if I hear scratching near a wall or behind something, my body just reacts before I can even think it through. Or if I see something move fast by a pipe, even if I’m not sure what it was, I get that same rush of fear immediately.\n\nIt’s like my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel this urgent need to get away from that spot. I don’t really pause and say, “Okay, let’s figure out what that was.” I just feel like I have to get out first, and then maybe think after. That’s the part that makes me feel kind of ridiculous, because sometimes it might be nothing, but in the moment my body is already acting like it’s a rat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself afraid of other small animals, like mice, in a similar way to your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t think it’s the same in my mind. It’s almost like my body decides it’s a rat before my mind has had a chance to catch up.\n\nSo I guess a picture isn’t the same as that. It’s the possibility of a live one being nearby, moving around, maybe coming out from somewhere — that’s what really scares me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of rats ever triggered by thinking about the environments or conditions where rats are typically found, even if you don't actually see a rat?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Honestly, that might be one of the worst parts for me, because I don’t even have to see one. I’ll stand there thinking about what could be behind something or under something, and I just can’t make myself go in or finish the area.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know those places aren’t automatically dangerous, but once I connect the place with rats, it feels like I’m already reacting as if one is about to come out.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that your fear is specifically of rats, but does the fear extend to other similar rodents like squirrels or gerbils?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in the same way. I mean, if a squirrel ran right near me unexpectedly I’d probably jump, and I don’t exactly want to hold a gerbil or anything, but that’s not what brought me here.\n\nIt’s rats. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that it’s interfering with my cleaning job. Like, I can be in an old building or a trash room, and even if part of me knows, “Okay, this is probably just a dirty area or an older place, it doesn’t mean there’s actually anything dangerous here,” I still can’t get past the thought that a rat might be there. And then I can’t do certain parts of the job, or I avoid that area, or I freeze up trying to make myself go in.\n\nSo with squirrels or gerbils, I don’t have that same reaction where it takes over and stops me from working. It’s really the rats — or even the thought that there could be rats — that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your personal relationships or daily activities outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I mean… it’s there some, but the biggest damage has really been around work, because that affects everything else, you know?
